Reduction from sparse LPN to LPN, Dual Attack 3.0 Thanks: The work of KC, TDA and JPT was funded by the French Agence Nationale de la Recherche through ANR JCJC DECODE (ANR-22-CE39-0004-01) for KC, ANR JCJC COLA (ANR-21-CE39-0011) for TDA and ANR-22-PETQ-0008 PQ-TLS for JPT. The work of CMH was funded by the French Agence de l’innovation de défense and by Inria.
Kévin Carrier 1, Thomas Debris–Alazard 2,3, Charles Meyer-Hilfiger 4, Jean-Pierre Tillich 4

Abstract
. The security of code-based cryptography relies primarily on the hardness of decoding generic linear codes. Until very recently, all the best algorithms for solving the decoding problem were information set decoders ( 𝖨𝖲𝖣 \mathsf{ISD} ). However, recently a new algorithm called 𝖱𝖫𝖯𝖭 \mathsf{RLPN} -decoding which relies on a completely different approach was introduced and it has been shown that 𝖱𝖫𝖯𝖭 \mathsf{RLPN} outperforms significantly 𝖨𝖲𝖣 \mathsf{ISD} decoders for a rather large range of rates. This 𝖱𝖫𝖯𝖭 \mathsf{RLPN} decoder relies on two ingredients, first reducing decoding to some underlying 𝖫𝖯𝖭 \mathsf{LPN} problem, and then computing efficiently many parity-checks of small weight when restricted to some positions. We revisit 𝖱𝖫𝖯𝖭 \mathsf{RLPN} -decoding by noticing that, in this algorithm, decoding is in fact reduced to a sparse- 𝖫𝖯𝖭 \mathsf{LPN} problem, namely with a secret whose Hamming weight is small. Our new approach consists this time in making an additional reduction from sparse- 𝖫𝖯𝖭 \mathsf{LPN} to plain- 𝖫𝖯𝖭 \mathsf{LPN} with a coding approach inspired by 𝖼𝗈𝖽𝖾𝖽 \mathsf{coded} - 𝖡𝖪𝖶 \mathsf{BKW} . It outperforms significantly the 𝖨𝖲𝖣 \mathsf{ISD} ’s and 𝖱𝖫𝖯𝖭 \mathsf{RLPN} for code rates smaller than 0.42 0.42 . This algorithm can be viewed as the code-based cryptography cousin of recent dual attacks in lattice-based cryptography. We depart completely from the traditional analysis of this kind of algorithm which uses a certain number of independence assumptions that have been strongly questioned recently in the latter domain. We give instead a formula for the 𝖫𝖯𝖭 \mathsf{LPN} noise relying on duality which allows to analyze the behavior of the algorithm by relying only on the analysis of a certain weight distribution. By using only a minimal assumption whose validity has been verified experimentally we are able to justify the correctness of our algorithm. This key tool, namely the duality formula, can be readily adapted to the lattice setting and is shown to give a simple explanation for some phenomena observed on dual attacks in lattices in [ DP23b ] .



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

Code-based Cryptography: Decoding and
\mathsf{LPN}
Problems.

Code-based cryptography relies on the hardness of decoding generic linear codes or sometimes also on a closely related problem, namely the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem. The first one corresponds in the binary case to

Problem 1

(decoding a fixed error weight in a linear code)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a binary linear code over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
of dimension
k
and length
n
, i.e. a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
of dimension
k
.
We are given
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
, an integer
t
and we want to find a codeword
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}
and an error vector
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
of Hamming weight
|\mathbf{e}|=t
for which
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{e}
.

Generally the linear code is specified by a
generator matrix
, namely a
k\times n
binary matrix
\mathbf{G}
whose
rows span the vector space
\mathcal{C}
, in other words

\mathcal{C}=\{\mathbf{u}\mathbf{G}:\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\}.

The second one is a version of this problem where the length
n
is basically unbounded; the code is randomly chosen and the
error model is slightly modified to take into account that the length is not fixed.

Problem 2

(
\mathsf{LPN}
problem)
.

Let
\mathbf{s}
be a secret chosen uniformly at random in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
. We have unbounded access to an oracle such that each query provides a pair

(\mathbf{a},b)
where
\mathbf{a}
is chosen uniformly at random in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
and
b
is a bit obtained as

b=\left\langle\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right\rangle+e

where
e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
is chosen at random and is equal to
1
with probability
p
. Quantity
\left\langle\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\right\rangle
stands for the inner product

\sum_{i=1}^{k}s_{i}a_{i}
between
\mathbf{s}=(s_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k}
. The aim is to output
\mathbf{s}
after querying
a certain number of times the oracle.

Sometimes a variation of the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem is considered, namely the
sparse
\mathsf{LPN}
problem where the only difference is the way
\mathbf{s}
is chosen, say uniformly at random among the words of length
n
and Hamming weight
t^{\prime}
small, or the entries like i.i.d. Bernoulli random
variables of parameter
p^{\prime}
small.

The Complexity of the Best Generic Decoding Algorithms and
\mathsf{LPN}
-solvers.

It is of fundamental importance to study the complexity of these problems, the best state of the art algorithms being those that are used to
determine secure parameters of code-based cryptosystems. The regime of parameters which is relevant for code-based cryptography depends on the type of primitive, but a large range of parameters is relevant here. For some code-based cryptosystems,
t
is sublinear in
n
,
[
McE78
,
AAB+21a
,
AAB+21b
,
BCL+19
,
AAB+21b
]
, for some Stern like signatures schemes

[
Ste93
,
Vér96
,
CVA10
,
AGS11
,
GPS22
,
FJR22
]
it is precisely decoding at the Gilbert-Varshamov distance that is relevant. It is at this distance that the decoding problem is expected to be the hardest. Recall that the Gilbert-Varshamov distance
d_{\mathrm{GV}}(n,k)
is given by

d_{\mathrm{GV}}(n,k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}n\;h^{-1}(1-R)
, where
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{k}{n}
is the code rate,
h
is the binary entropy function
h(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}-x\log_{2}x-(1-x)\log_{2}(1-x)
and
h^{-1}(x)
its inverse ranging over
\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
.
Above this bound, the number of solutions becomes exponential and this helps to devise more efficient decoders.

Concerning now the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem, it has long been recognized that having an unbounded number of queries or codelength while having a fixed
error probability
p
per bit as in
\mathsf{LPN}
makes the problem really simpler. The best algorithms for solving this problem,
are
\mathsf{BKW}
type algorithms
[
BKW03
,
EKM17
]
and are of subexponential complexity
2^{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(k/\log k\right)}
.
However, this is not true anymore if the number of queries is fixed and the error rate
p
is chosen such that the problem is the hardest, namely when
h(p)=1-k/n
. In this case, the best algorithms behave exponentially in
\min(k,n-k)

despite many efforts on this issue

[
Pra62
,
Ste88
,
Dum91
,
BLP11
,
MMT11
,
BJMM12
,
MO15
,
BM17
,
BM18
,
CDMT22
]
.

Reduction from Decoding to an
\mathsf{LPN}
Problem.

Note that until very recently, all the best algorithms for solving the decoding problem or the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem when it is the hardest have been
\mathsf{ISD}
algorithms. They all rely crucially on the Prange bet, namely that we have finally found after many trials a subset of positions of size
\approx n-k
which contains almost all the errors. This was the situation since 1962
[
Pra62
]
.
There was at some point, just one exception
[
Dum86
]
which relied instead on a collision technique and gave only a slight improvement in a very tiny rate range
R\in(0.98,1)
, but it was soon found out how to incorporate this
technique in
\mathsf{ISD}
algorithms
[
Ste88
,
Dum89
]
to improve them.
However in 2022, a new algorithm called
\mathsf{RLPN}
-decoding was introduced in
[
CDMT22
]
. It relies on a completely different approach following an old idea called “statistical decoding” due to Al Jabri
[
Jab01
]
. The new approach consists in reducing decoding to
\mathsf{LPN}
. For the first time in sixty years a strong competitor for
\mathsf{ISD}
techniques was found: it outperforms
\mathsf{ISD}
techniques in the low rate regime, say
R\in(0,0.3)
and the improvement is quite significant in the range
R\in(0,0.2)
say. To explain the idea, assume we are given an instance of the decoding problem
{\mathbf{y}}={\mathbf{c}}+{\mathbf{e}}
, where
{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{C}
and
|{\mathbf{e}}|=t
.
As in statistical decoding, decoding relies on low weight parity-check equations, namely vectors
\mathbf{h}
such that
\left\langle\mathbf{h},\mathbf{c}\right\rangle=0
for any

\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}
(in other words, such
\mathbf{h}
’s belong to the dual code
\mathcal{C}^{\perp}
). However, in the new approach these parity-check equations
are required to be of low weight only on a subset
{\mathscr{N}}
of positions. The rest of the positions
{\mathscr{P}}
correspond to the entries of
\mathbf{e}
we aim to recover and is the secret in the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem. The point of the whole approach is that

\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{h}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{e},\mathbf{h}\rangle=\sum_{j\in{\mathscr{P}}}h_{j}e_{j}+\sum_{j\in{\mathscr{N}}}h_{j}e_{j}=\underbrace{\langle\mathbf{e}_{{\mathscr{P}}},\mathbf{h}_{{\mathscr{P}}}\rangle}_{\text{lin. comb.}}+\underbrace{\langle\mathbf{e}_{{\mathscr{N}}},\mathbf{h}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\rangle}_{\text{$\mathsf{LPN}${} noise}}.

Here the notation
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
means the restriction of
\mathbf{e}
to the positions in
{\mathscr{P}}
:

\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}=(e_{i})_{i\in{\mathscr{P}}}
. Vector
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
is interpreted as the
\mathsf{LPN}
secret
\mathbf{s}
,
i.e.
\mathbf{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
and
\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{P}}
as the linear
combination vector
\mathbf{a}
while
\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{N}}\right\rangle
is the
\mathsf{LPN}
noise. Therefore, by computing (
\mathbf{h},\left\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{h}\right\rangle)
we really have access to the
\mathsf{LPN}
sample

\underbrace{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{P}}},\overbrace{\underbrace{\langle\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\rangle}_{\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{P}}\right\rangle}+\underbrace{e}_{\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{N}}\right\rangle}}^{=\left\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{h}\right\rangle}.

The point of choosing low weight vectors
\mathbf{h}
on
{\mathscr{N}}
, is that it is readily verified that this translates into the fact that the binary
random variable
\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{N}}\right\rangle
is biased, say
\Prob(\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{N}}\right\rangle=1)=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}
with
a bias
\varepsilon
which gets bigger when the Hamming weight
|\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{N}}|
of
\mathbf{h}
on
{\mathscr{N}}
gets smaller.

Recovering
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
is then performed by producing enough parity-check equations to have enough information on
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
(we need about
N\approx 1/\varepsilon^{2}
parity-check equations) and amounts to solve the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem. This is done by the Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) and costs about
s2^{s}
where

s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\left|{\mathscr{P}}\right|
. We cannot afford more sophisticated techniques like the
\mathsf{BKW}
algorithm which would give a sub-exponential algorithm, because we are very far away from the constant error probability regime. Here the bias
\varepsilon
is exponentially small in the codelength, so we are really in the extreme noise regime, where on top of that we have hardly more
\mathsf{LPN}
samples than the number we need to recover the secret. In other words, we are in a situation where we can only use very basic algorithms, and the FFT which saves a factor
N
when compared to plain exhaustive search over all possible
\mathsf{LPN}
secrets comes in handy here. The low weight parity-check equations are found by using collision techniques which are borrowed from advanced
\mathsf{ISD}
techniques
[
Dum89
,
BJMM12
]
.

The improvement upon statistical decoding given by
\mathsf{RLPN}
is really due to this splitting in two parts. Recall that plain statistical decoding uses parity-checks which are low weight on the
whole support
. In both cases,
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}
of such parity-checks are needed, however in
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoding the bias
\varepsilon
is way bigger because the weight we have on
{\mathscr{N}}
is way smaller for our parity-checks.

Dual Attacks, Some Negative Results and a New Analysis.

Statistical decoding
[
Jab01
]
or its variant, namely
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoding, both fall into the category of
dual attacks
meaning a decoding algorithm that computes in a first step low weight codewords
in the dual code and then computes the inner products of the received word
{\mathbf{y}}
with those parity-checks to infer some information about the error
{\mathbf{e}}
. These methods can be viewed as the coding theoretic analogue of the dual attacks in lattice-based cryptography
[
MR09
]
. Similarly to what happened in code-based cryptography, they were shown after a sequence of improvements
[
Alb17
,
EJK20
,
GJ21
,
MAT22
,
CST22
]
to be able of being competitive with primal attacks, and the crucial improvement came from similar techniques, namely by a splitting strategy.
Like in
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoding, the point is that this splitting in two parts really allows to find dual vectors that are of smaller weight/norm on the restricted subset. Note that this idea was already put forward for statistical decoding (but not exploited there) in
[
DT17a
, §8, p.33]
or
[
DT17b
, p. 21]
.

However, the analysis in both settings relies on various independence assumptions, see for instance
[
MAT22
, Ass. 4.4, Ass. 5.8]
for dual attacks in lattices or
[
CDMT22
, Ass. 3.7]
for dual attacks for codes. In lattice-based cryptography, the dual attacks were strongly questioned recently in
[
DP23b
]
by showing that
these independence assumptions made for analyzing dual attacks were in contradiction with some theorems in certain regimes or with well-tested heuristics in some other regimes. Note that it was already noticed in
[
CDMT22
, §3.4]
that the i.i.d. Bernoulli model implied by the
\mathsf{LPN}
model for the
\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\rangle
’s is not always accurate, but it was conjectured there that the discrepancy between this ideal model and experiments does not impact the asymptotic analysis of the decoding based on this model. This was proved to be wrong in
[
MT23
]
where it was shown that the number of candidates passing the validity test of the
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoder given in
[
CDMT22
]
is actually exponentially large for the parameters considered there, whereas there should be only one candidate passing the test if the algorithm was correct. However, this paper gave at the same time an approach for analyzing rigorously dual attacks in coding theory by bringing in a duality equation
[
MT23
, Prop. 1.3]
which relates the fundamental quantity manipulated by the decoder and the weight distribution of translates of a shortened version of the code to be decoded. By studying this weight distribution together with an assumption whose validity has been verified experimentally, a slightly modified
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoder was introduced there and shown to attain the complexity exponent claimed in
[
CDMT22
]
.

1.2.
Our Contribution

(i)

improving
\mathsf{RLPN}
-decoding by a reduction from sparse
\mathsf{LPN}
to plain
\mathsf{LPN}
,

(ii)

a rigorous analysis of the decoding algorithm based on a simple assumption verified experimentally.

Reduction from Sparse
\mathsf{LPN}
to Plain
\mathsf{LPN}
.
Notice that the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem we have to solve is actually a
sparse

\mathsf{LPN}
problem:
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
is not uniformly distributed among
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s}
since it is of low weight. Indeed, it is the restriction to
{\mathscr{P}}
of a vector which is itself of low weight. Unfortunately,
the FFT algorithm used for recovering
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
is unable to exploit this fact. In a sense, what we need here to improve
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoding is an algorithm for solving sparse secret
\mathsf{LPN}
in the very noisy regime (but with an exponential number of samples). This can be
done by using a
\mathsf{coded}
-
\mathsf{BKW}
technique that was introduced in
[
GJL14
]
. There it was not used as a technique for solving sparse
\mathsf{LPN}
but as a technique to improve the reduction steps of the
\mathsf{BKW}
algorithm
[
BKW03
]
that put together pairs of vectors
\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
which are equal on a block of positions and add the corresponding
\mathsf{LPN}
samples to get an
\mathsf{LPN}
sample
(\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\left\langle\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{a}^{\prime},\mathbf{s}\right\rangle+e+e^{\prime})
which is more noisy but with vectors
\mathbf{a}
which become sparser and sparser as the number of blocks increases. Asking exact collisions on the block needs a lot of
\mathsf{LPN}
samples and this can be relaxed by the
\mathsf{coded}
-
\mathsf{BKW}
technique. It basically uses a code of the same length as the block of positions we are considering during the
\mathsf{BKW}
step and asks only an approximate collision on the block meaning that the closest codewords
\mathbf{c}
and
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
restricted to this block should be the same.

To explain what we have in mind here, consider an
\mathsf{LPN}

sample
which is of the following form
(\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{P}},\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{P}}\right\rangle+e)
. Choose now a linear code
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
of length
s
and dimension
{k_{\textup{aux}}}
(
i.e.
a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s}
) which we know how to decode for any possible entry, meaning here that we can produce for any entry
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s}
a codeword
{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\in{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
which is close
enough
to
{\mathbf{y}}
.
Codes with this property are known under the name of
lossy source codes
in information theory. In
[
GJL14
]
it was proposed to use for instance a product of small codes.
There are almost optimal codes (producing for a given dimension
{k_{\textup{aux}}}
almost optimal near codewords) using a low complexity decoder. Basically, the best that can be done is to produce codewords at distance
d_{\mathrm{GV}}(s,{k_{\textup{aux}}})
.
For instance polar codes are asymptotically optimal
[
KU10
]
, they attain asymptotically this Gilbert-Varshamov distance by using only a decoding algorithm of quasi-linear complexity
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(s\log s\right)
.

Consider now a parity-check
\mathbf{h}
of small weight
w
on
{\mathscr{N}}
that we use for
\mathsf{RLPN}
-decoding and decode
\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{P}}
with the lossy source code
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
:
\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{P}}={{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}+{{\mathbf{e}}_{\textup{aux}}}
where
{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\in{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
and
|{{\mathbf{e}}_{\textup{aux}}}|
is small. Consider a generator matrix
{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}
of
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
, namely a
{k_{\textup{aux}}}\times s
matrix such that

{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}=\{\mathbf{u}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}:\;\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{k_{\textup{aux}}}}\}
(
i.e.
the rows of
{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}
generate
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
). Notice now that

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{P}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}+{{\mathbf{e}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},{{\mathbf{e}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},\mathbf{u}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},{{\mathbf{e}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle\;\;\text{(where $\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{k_{\textup{aux}}}}$)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle+\underbrace{\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},{{\mathbf{e}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle}_{\text{biased}}.

If we plug this expression in the original
\mathsf{LPN}
sample
(\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{P}},\left\langle\mathbf{h},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{P}}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{N}}\right\rangle)
we obtain

\left\langle\mathbf{h},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle+\underbrace{\left\langle\mathbf{e},{{\mathbf{e}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle}_{\text{noise 1}}+\underbrace{\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{N}}\right\rangle}_{\text{noise 2}}.

In other words, we have a new
\mathsf{LPN}
problem where

(1)

\underbrace{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathbf{u}},\overbrace{\underbrace{\langle\mathbf{s},\mathbf{a}\rangle}_{\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle}+\underbrace{e}_{\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},{{\mathbf{e}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{N}}\right\rangle}}^{\left\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{h}\right\rangle}.

The new secret is not anymore a part
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
of the error but a linear combination
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
of it and the
\mathsf{LPN}
noise has increased somehow. However, now the secret is way smaller, it belongs to
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{k_{\textup{aux}}}}
. The situation is changed significantly by this. Before, basically the optimal parameters for
\mathsf{RLPN}
-decoding were such that the cost of FFT decoding the
\mathsf{LPN}
secret, namely
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(s2^{s}\right)
is of the same order as
1/\varepsilon^{2}
the number of parity-check equations we need. Here
\varepsilon
is defined by

\Prob(e=1)=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}.

Recall that
\varepsilon
is basically a decreasing function of the weight
w
of the parity-check equations we are able to produce.
Here since we do not pay anymore
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(s2^{s}\right)
for
FFT decoding the new
\mathsf{LPN}
secret but
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left({k_{\textup{aux}}}2^{{k_{\textup{aux}}}}\right)
we can take larger values for
s
which themselves give a smaller support
{\mathscr{N}}
resulting in much smaller weight
w
on
{\mathscr{N}}
and thus the bias term coming from
\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{N}},\mathbf{h}_{\mathscr{N}}\right\rangle
is much smaller. Of course there is an additional noise term now which is
\left\langle\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}},{{\mathbf{e}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle
. However, all in all, the gain we have by being able to use a much larger
s
outweighs the additional noise term. It can also be observed that we do not recover
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
but
{k_{\textup{aux}}}
linear combinations of bits of
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
. This is easy to fix by running a few times more this algorithm with other lossy source codes
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
until getting enough linear combinations to be able to recover
\mathbf{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
.

We call this new algorithm
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
-decoding, since it is based on two successive reductions: first we reduce the problem to sparse-
\mathsf{LPN}
, then we reduce the sparse-
\mathsf{LPN}
to a plain-
\mathsf{LPN}
problem as explained above.

\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
-Decoding and its Analysis.

It turns out that the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem given in Equation (
1
) is more structured than a standard
\mathsf{LPN}
problem
and like what happened in the
\mathsf{RLPN}
algorithm
[
CDMT22
]
, producing the most likely candidate for the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem does not necessarily produce the right candidate
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
even if we have enough samples for ensuring that in the ideal i.i.d model of the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem the most likely candidate would indeed be the right solution. Again, the i.i.d. model is not accurate. We have to use the whole information given by the FFT and output for
L
big enough the
L
most likely solutions to have a chance to have
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
in the list. However, verifying whether a candidate
{\mathbf{s}}
for

{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
is indeed valid is relatively straightforward:

(a)

we can as in the
\mathsf{RLPN}
algorithm make a bet on the weight
|{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}|
, say

|{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}|=t^{\prime}
(and run enough
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoding steps until finding a partition
{\mathscr{P}}\cup{\mathscr{N}}
for which this bet is valid),

(b)

recover
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}
by solving the decoding problem (in its syndrome form)
{\mathbf{s}}={\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
and
|{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}|=t^{\prime}
,

(c)

check whether the putative candidate
{\mathbf{v}}
for
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}
we get can be extended to a complete solution by solving the decoding problem

{\mathbf{y}}={\mathbf{c}}+{\mathbf{e}}
,
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}={\mathbf{v}}
,
{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{C}
and
|{\mathbf{e}}|=t
which is much easier to solve than the original decoding problem due to the partial knowledge
about
{\mathbf{e}}
,
i.e.
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}={\mathbf{v}}
.

The whole problem we face here for analyzing the problem is the same as the one that was faced to analyze the
\mathsf{RLPN}
algorithm, the i.i.d.
\mathsf{LPN}
model
is not valid and we really have to get rid of the independence assumptions. Part of this work is achieved
by adapting one of the fundamental tools used for analyzing
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoding, namely
[
CDMT22
, Proposition 3.1]
which gives a formula of the bias
\varepsilon
in terms of Krawtchouk polynomials. We will obtain a generalization of this proposition adapted to the
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoder, namely Proposition
2
in §
3
. Note that Proposition
2
does not rely on unproven assumptions contrarily to what is done in dual attacks in lattice-based cryptography where
the corresponding result is achieved through independence assumptions.

Estimating the number
L
of candidates for the
\mathsf{LPN}
problem given in Equation (
1
) we have to keep for being sure to have
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
is even more delicate. It requires a careful adaptation to our setting of
[
MT23
]
that analyzed the
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoder.
Here, we will not be able to avoid completely assumptions for performing the analysis (but this was also the case in
[
MT23
]
). However, again we will not resort to independence assumptions which seem in our context not only to be wrong strictly speaking, but also to be unable to be good enough for capturing the size of
L
. We will namely develop some tools analogous to what has been achieved in
[
MT23
]
:

(i)

a duality result, namely Proposition
4
of §
5.3
, which expresses the FFT value of a candidate as a
weighted sum of the product of evaluations of Krawtchouk polynomials where the weights come from a certain weight distributions of codes related to
\mathcal{C}
and
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
. This is an adaptation of
[
MT23
, Prop. 3.2]
to our setting and is the key for estimating
L
as explained in §
5.2
,

(ii)

an estimation of this sum with probabilistic considerations. These probabilistic considerations are rigorous for the part of the sum which is most certainly the dominating term. However for the part of the sum which is very likely to be negligible, we lack accurate tail bounds for the number of codewords of a given weight in a random linear code and in this case we just conjecture that the part of the sum which seems negligible and for which we have only partial control with the probabilistic tool at hand, is indeed negligible. This conjecture has been verified experimentally and we even used a very crude approximation of this weighted sum with the help of independent Poisson variables which captures the size of
L
obtained in our experiments and which implies our conjecture.

All in all with the help of a conjecture that we verified experimentally, we are able to capture the size of
L
and to obtain a formula for the complexity of
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoding. The key tool for performing this analysis, namely the duality result, can be readily adapted to lattices (see §
8
). It turns out that even a crude use of this duality result gives a good explanation of the part of the experimental curve departing from the theoretical curve based on the standard independence assumption found in
[
DP23b
, Fig. 3]
. This substantiates the claim made in
[
MT23
, §6]
that the code duality result of
[
MT23
]
carries over to the lattice setting and can be used to predict dual attacks without using the independence assumption.

The Results Obtained by this New Approach.

This new approach results in a very significant gain compared to
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoding. Our most advanced version of
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
-decoding algorithm performs better than the current state of the art
\mathsf{ISD}
algorithm for all rates
R\leqslant 0.42
as shown in Figure
1
.

Figure 1
.
Asymptotic complexity exponent of some decoding algorithms:
our new
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoder,
the
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoder,
Both and May algorithm
[
BM18
]
(with the correction of
[
CDMT22
,
Ess22
]
) which is the state-of-the-art of
\mathsf{ISD}
decoders and the Prange decoder
[
Pra62
]
.

Concurrent/related work.

Very recently, we became aware that the prediction we have made on the score function for lattices by using our duality result and crude estimates of the relevant sum
(see §
8
) has also been obtained by using as we do here Bessel functions and related tools in
[
DP23a
]
. This paper provides a much more in depth study as we do here.

2.
Notation and Coding Theory Background

Basic Notation.

Vectors and matrices are respectively denoted in bold letters
and bold capital letters such as
{\mathbf{a}}
and
{\mathbf{A}}
. The entry at index
i
of the vector
{\mathbf{x}}
is denoted by
x_{i}
or
x(i)
. The canonical inner product
\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}
between two vectors
{\mathbf{x}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}
of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
is denoted by
\left\langle{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\right\rangle
where
\mathbb{F}_{2}
denotes the binary field.
Let
{\mathscr{I}}
be a list of indexes. We denote by
{\mathbf{x}}_{{\mathscr{I}}}
the vector
(x_{i})_{i\in{\mathscr{I}}}
. In the same way, we denote by
{\mathbf{A}}_{{\mathscr{I}}}
the sub-matrix made of the columns of
{\mathbf{A}}
which are indexed by
{\mathscr{I}}
. We denote by
\mathbf{0}_{n}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n\times n}
and
\mathbf{Id}_{n}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n\times n}
the null matrix and the identity matrix of size
n
respectively.
The concatenation of two vectors
{\mathbf{x}}
and
{\mathbf{y}}
is denoted by
{\mathbf{x}}||{\mathbf{y}}
.
The Hamming weight of a vector
{\mathbf{x}}
and the cardinality of a finite set
{\mathscr{A}}
are denoted in the same way by
|{\mathbf{x}}|
and
\left|{\mathscr{A}}\right|
respectively. There will be no confusion since they apply to different objects.
Notation
\left\llbracket a,b\right\rrbracket
stands for the set of the integers between
a
and
b
, both included.
Furthermore, we let
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{n}
denote the Hamming sphere of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
with radius
w
and centered at
\mathbf{0}
, namely

\mathcal{S}_{w}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\{{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\>:|{\mathbf{x}}|=w\}.

Probabilistic Notation.

For a finite set
{\mathscr{S}}
, we write
X{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}}{\mathscr{S}}
when
X
is an element of
{\mathscr{S}}
drawn uniformly at random in it.
For a Bernoulli random variable
X
, denote by
\bias(X)
the quantity

\bias(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\Prob(X=0)-\Prob(X=1).

For a Bernoulli random variable
X
of parameter
p=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}
,
i.e.
\Prob(X=1)=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}
, we have
\bias(X)=\varepsilon
.

Fourier Transform.

Let
f\>:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function. We define its Fourier transform
\widehat{f}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as

(2)

\widehat{f}\left({\mathbf{x}}\right)=\sum_{{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}f({\mathbf{u}})\left(-1\right)^{\left\langle{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{u}}\right\rangle}.

Soft-O Notation.

For real valued functions defined over
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{N}
we define
o()
,
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(\right)
,
\Omega\left(\right)
,
\Theta\left(\right)
, in the usual way and also use the less common notation
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\left(\right)
and
\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\right)
, where

f=\mathop{}\mathopen{}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\left(g\right)
means that
f(x)=\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(g(x)\log^{k}g(x)\right)
and
f=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(g\right)
means that
f(x)=\Omega\left(g(x)\log^{k}g(x)\right)
for some
k
. We will use this
for functions which have an exponential behavior, say
g(x)=e^{\alpha x}
, in which case
f(x)=\mathop{}\mathopen{}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\left(g(x)\right)
means that

f(x)=\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(P(x)g(x)\right)
where
P
is a polynomial in
x
.
We also use
f=\omega(g)
when
f
dominates
g
asymptotically; that is when
\mathop{\lim}\limits_{x\rightarrow\infty}\frac{|f(x)|}{g(x)}=\infty
.

Coding Theory.
A binary linear code
\mathcal{C}
of length
n
and dimension
k
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
of dimension
k
. We say that it has parameters
[n,k]
or that it is an
[n,k]
-code. Its
rate

R
is defined as
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{k}{n}
.
A generator matrix
{\mathbf{G}}
for
\mathcal{C}
is a full rank
k\times n
matrix over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
such that

\mathcal{C}=\left\{{\mathbf{u}}{\mathbf{G}}:{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\right\}.

A parity-check matrix
{\mathbf{H}}
for
\mathcal{C}
is a full-rank
(n-k)\times n
matrix over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
such that

\mathcal{C}=\left\{{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}:{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{c}}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{0}\right\}.

In other words,
\mathcal{C}
is the null space of
{\mathbf{H}}
. The dimension of the code is given by
\dim\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}k
.
The code whose generator matrix is the parity-check matrix of
\mathcal{C}
is called the dual code of
\mathcal{C}
. It might be seen as the subspace of parity-checks of
\mathcal{C}
and is defined equivalently as

Definition 1

(Dual Code)
.

The dual code
\mathcal{C}^{\perp}
of an
[n,k]
-code
\mathcal{C}
is an
[n,n-k]
-code which is defined by

\mathcal{C}^{\perp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\left\{\mathbf{h}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}:\forall{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{C},\;\left\langle{\mathbf{c}},\mathbf{h}\right\rangle=0\right\}.

Sometimes it is considered in the literature the following equivalent version of the decoding problem (see Problem
3
as defined in the introduction) by using instead the parity-check matrix and syndrome point of view

Problem 3

(Decoding a fixed error weight via syndromes)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
[n,k]
-code with parity-check matrix
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{(n-k)\times n}
.
We are given a syndrome
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}
, an integer
t
and we want to find an error vector
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
of Hamming weight
|\mathbf{e}|=t
for which
\mathbf{H}\mathbf{e}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}
.

It is readily seen that both Problems
1
and
3
are equivalent: given
\mathcal{C}
with parity-check matrix
\mathbf{H}
, then decoding
\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{e}
with a codeword
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}
and
\mathbf{e}\in\mathcal{S}_{t}^{n}
amounts to recover
\mathbf{e}
from
\mathbf{H}\mathbf{y}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{H}\mathbf{e}^{\intercal}
as by definition
\mathbf{H}\mathbf{c}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{0}
.

When
\mathcal{C}
is an
[n,k]
-code and
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
we let

\mathcal{C}+{\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\{{\mathbf{c}}+{\mathbf{x}}\>,{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{C}\}

denote a coset of
\mathcal{C}
and we denote by
N_{i}\left(\mathcal{C}+{\mathbf{x}}\right)
the number of words of hamming weight
i
in the coset
\mathcal{C}+{\mathbf{x}}
, namely

N_{i}\left(\mathcal{C}+{\mathbf{x}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\left|\mathcal{C}+{\mathbf{x}}\cap\mathcal{S}_{i}^{n}\right|.

An important quantity is the
Gilbert-Varshamov
distance which is defined as

Definition 2

(Gilbert-Varshamov distance)
.

The Gilbert-Varshamov distance
d_{\mathrm{GV}}(n,k)
associated to a length
n
and dimension
k
is defined as
the largest integer
d
such that

2^{k}\left|{\mathscr{B}}_{d}\right|<2^{n}

where
{\mathscr{B}}_{d}
is the Hamming ball centered at
\mathbf{0}
in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
and radius
d
, that is

{\mathscr{B}}_{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\{{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}:\;|{\mathbf{x}}|\leqslant d\}
.

This quantity has two different interpretations. On one hand, it corresponds up to a constant term to the
typical minimum distance
of a linear code of length
n
and dimension
k
, but it is also related to the expected number of solutions of the decoding problem for a random linear
[n,k]
-code which is defined as follows.

Problem 4

(
(n,k,t)
Decoding Problem -
\mathsf{DP}(n,k,t)
)
.

•

Given:

(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathbf{m}\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{x})
where
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{x}
are respectively picked uniformly at random over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
,
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k\times n}
and
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{n}
.

•

Aim:
an error
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
of Hamming weight
t
such that
\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{z}\mathbf{G}
for some
\mathbf{z} \in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
.

This problem really corresponds to decoding at distance
t
the
[n,k]
-code admitting
\mathbf{G}
as generator matrix.
The largest weight
t
for which we might hope for having a single solution (strictly speaking when we look for solutions of weight
\leqslant t
and not exactly
t
, but the difference
between these two notions is generally irrelevant) is given by the Gilbert-Varshamov distance
d_{\mathrm{GV}}(n,k)
. At this distance, the expected number of solutions
is readily seen to be
\Theta\left(1\right)
whether we look
at codewords at distance exactly
t
from the received word
{\mathbf{y}}
or
at distance
\leqslant t
.

It will also be very convenient to consider the operation of puncturing a code,
i.e.
keeping only a subset of entries in a codeword.

Definition 3

(Punctured Code)
.

For a code
\mathcal{C}
and a subset
{\mathscr{I}}
of code positions, we denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathscr{I}}
the punctured code obtained from
\mathcal{C}
by keeping only the positions in
{\mathscr{I}}
, i.e.

\mathcal{C}_{{\mathscr{I}}}=\{{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathscr{I}}:{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{C}\}.

Definition 4

(Shortened Code)
.

For a code
\mathcal{C}
and a subset
{\mathscr{I}}
of code positions, we denote by
\mathcal{C}^{\mathscr{I}}
the shortened code is defined by

\mathcal{C}^{{\mathscr{I}}}=\{{\mathbf{c}}_{\mathscr{I}}:{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{C}\mbox{ and }{\mathbf{c}}_{\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\setminus{\mathscr{I}}}=\mathbf{0}\}.

It is readily seen that we have

(3)

\left(\mathcal{C}^{{\mathscr{I}}}\right)^{\perp}=\left(\mathcal{C}^{\perp}\right)_{{\mathscr{I}}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left(\mathcal{C}_{{\mathscr{I}}}\right)^{\perp}=\left(\mathcal{C}^{\perp}\right)^{{\mathscr{I}}}.

Krawtchouk Polynomial.

We recall here some properties about Krawtchouk polynomial that will be useful in the article. Many useful properties can be found in
[
KS21
, §2.2]

Definition 5
.

(Krawtchouk polynomial)
We define the Krawtchouk polynomial
K_{w}^{(n)}
of degree
w
and of order
n
as

K_{w}^{(n)}\left(X\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{j=0}^{w}\left(-1\right)^{j}\binom{X}{j}\binom{n-X}{w-j}.

The following fact is well known: it gives an alternate expression of the Krawtchouk polynomial (see for instance
[
vL99
, Lemma 5.3.1]
).

Fact 1
.

For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
,

(4)

K_{w}^{(n)}\left(|{\mathbf{x}}|\right)=\widehat{{\mathbbm{1}}_{w}}({\mathbf{x}})=\sum_{{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}:|{\mathbf{y}}|=w}\left(-1\right)^{\langle{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}\rangle}.

where
{\mathbbm{1}}_{w}
is the characteristic function of the Hamming sphere
\mathcal{S}^{n}_{w}
of radius
w
.

We recall here the summary of some known results about Krawtchouk polynomials made in
[
CDMT22
]
.

Proposition 1
.

[
CDMT22
, Prop. 3.5, Prop. 3.6]

(1)

Value at 0.
For all
0\leqslant w\leqslant n
,
K_{w}^{(n)}(0)=\binom{n}{w}
.

(2)

Reciprocity.
For all
0\leqslant t,w\leqslant n
,
\binom{n}{t}K_{w}^{(n)}(t)=\binom{n}{w}K_{t}^{(n)}(w)
.

(3)

Roots.
The polynomials
K_{w}^{(n)}
’s have
w
distinct roots which lie in the interval

\llbracket n/2-\sqrt{w(n-w)},n/2+\sqrt{w(n-w)}\rrbracket.
The distance between roots is at least
2
and at most
o(n)
.

(4)

Magnitude in and out the root region.
Let
\tau
and
\omega
be two reals in
[0,1]
. Let
\omega^{\perp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}-\sqrt{\omega(1-\omega)}
, and let
z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{1-2\tau-\sqrt{D}}{2(1-\omega)}
where
D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\left(1-2\tau\right)^{2}-4\omega(1-\omega)
.

Define
\widetilde{\kappa}(\tau,\omega)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tau\log_{2}(1-z)+(1-\tau)\log_{2}(1+z)-\omega\log_{2}z&\mbox{ if }\tau\in[0,\omega^{\perp}],\\
\frac{1-h(\tau)+h(\omega)}{2}&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

•

4.1. If
\tau\leqslant\frac{1}{2}-\sqrt{\omega(1-\omega)}
, then for all
t
and
w
such that
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{t}{n}=\tau
and
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{w}{n}=\omega
we have
K_{w}^{(n)}(t)=2^{n\left(\widetilde{\kappa}\left(\tau,\omega\right)+o(1)\right)}
.

•

4.2. If
\tau>\frac{1}{2}-\sqrt{\omega(1-\omega)}
, then there exists
t(n)
and
w(n)
such that
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{t}{n}=\tau
,
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{w}{n}=\omega
and
\left|K_{w}^{(n)}(t)\right|=2^{n\left(\widetilde{\kappa}\left(\tau,\omega\right)+o(1)\right)}
.

3.
Reduction from Sparse to Plain
\mathsf{LPN}

The purpose of this section is to explain in detail the reduction from sparse to plain
\mathsf{LPN}
and to give an important result about the bias of the resulting
\mathsf{RLPN}
samples.
We assume from now on that we are given and
[n,k]
-code
\mathcal{C}
and a
{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
such that

{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathbf{c}}+{\mathbf{e}},\quad{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{C},\;|{\mathbf{e}}|=t,

and we want to find
{\mathbf{c}}
and
{\mathbf{e}}
.

3.1.
The Approach

First, we randomly select a subset
{\mathscr{P}}\subseteq\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
of
s
positions,
where
s
is a parameter that will be chosen later. Let
{\mathscr{N}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\setminus{\mathscr{P}}
be the complementary set of
{\mathscr{P}}
. Here
{\mathscr{P}}
corresponds to the entries of
{\mathbf{e}}
we aim to recover. As explained in the introduction, the basic step of the decoding algorithm is to compute a large set
{\mathscr{W}}
of parity-check equations of low weight
w
on
{\mathscr{N}}
and to compute all the

\left\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\right\rangle
with
{\mathbf{h}}
ranging over
{\mathscr{W}}
. In
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoding, the approach is
to exploit directly that we have a number
\left|{\mathscr{W}}\right|
of
\mathsf{LPN}
samples
({\mathbf{h}}_{\mathscr{P}},\left\langle{\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{y}}\right\rangle)
which can be viewed as an

\mathsf{LPN}
sample
\left({\mathbf{a}},\left\langle{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{s}}\right\rangle+e\right)
by letting
{\mathbf{a}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathbf{h}}_{\mathscr{P}}
,
{\mathbf{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}
,
e\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\left\langle{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\right\rangle
. Indeed,

\left\langle{\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{y}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{c}}+{\mathbf{e}}\right\rangle=\underbrace{\left\langle{\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{c}}\right\rangle}_{=0}+\left\langle{\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{e}}\right\rangle=\underbrace{\left\langle{\mathbf{h}}_{\mathscr{P}},{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}\right\rangle}_{=\left\langle{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{s}}\right\rangle}+\underbrace{\left\langle{\mathbf{h}}_{\mathscr{N}},{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\right\rangle}_{\text{$\mathsf{LPN}${} noise $e$}}.

Notice that we really have a sparse
\mathsf{LPN}
problem because of the sparseness of the secret
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}
which is not exploited in
[
CDMT22
]
and only exploited to verify the solution in the corrected

\mathsf{RLPN}
algorithm of
[
MT23
]
. The point of this article is to exploit the sparseness of
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s}
right away in order to reduce the dimension
s
of the secret. This is obtained by introducing an auxiliary code

{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
of length
s
and dimension
{k_{\textup{aux}}}
which will be instrumental for reducing the dimension
s
of the secret down to
{k_{\textup{aux}}}
. This is obtained as follows. We will assume that
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
is chosen as a code with an
efficient list-decoding procedure
at distance
{t_{\textup{aux}}}
.

Definition 6

(Efficiently list decodable code)
.

A code
\mathcal{C}
of length
n
is said to be efficiently decodable code at distance
t
if it outputs
for any
{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
a non empty list of codewords of
\mathcal{C}
at distance
t
in time
2^{o(n)}
.

Moreover from now on, we assume that

Notation 1
.

{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
is an
[s,{k_{\textup{aux}}}]
efficiently list decodable for some distance
{t_{\textup{aux}}}
. We denote by

{\mathcal{D}\mathrm{ec}}({\mathbf{z}})
the set of all codewords of
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
at distance
{t_{\textup{aux}}}
from
{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s}
, namely

{\mathcal{D}\mathrm{ec}}({\mathbf{z}})=\left\{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}\in{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}\;:\;\left|{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}+{\mathbf{z}}\right|={t_{\textup{aux}}}\right\}.

Remark 1
.

In our instantiation,
{t_{\textup{aux}}}
is chosen such that
{t_{\textup{aux}}}\approx d_{\mathrm{GV}}(s,{k_{\textup{aux}}})
, thus we typically have
\left|{\mathcal{D}\mathrm{ec}}({\mathbf{h}}_{\mathscr{P}})\right|=\Theta\left(1\right)
.

Now, let us consider
{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\in{\mathcal{D}\mathrm{ec}}({\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}})
, a codeword of
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
at distance
{t_{\textup{aux}}}
of
{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}
. It is readily seen that

\left\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\right\rangle
decomposes as:

\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\rangle=\underbrace{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}\rangle}_{\mbox{linear comb.}}+\underbrace{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}+{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\rangle}_{\mbox{``new'' $\mathsf{LPN}${} noise}}.

Let us start by defining
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
with a generator matrix
{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s\times{k_{\textup{aux}}}}
.
Then, knowing
{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}\in{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
is equivalent to know
{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{k_{\textup{aux}}}}
such that

{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}={{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}.

We can therefore rewrite
\left\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle
as

\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle=\left\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle=\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle.

We have therefore for each parity-check equation
{\mathbf{h}}
of weight
w
on
{\mathscr{N}}
that we have computed (
i.e.
for all
{\mathbf{h}}\in{\mathscr{W}}
) and each
codeword
{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}
of
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
at distance
{t_{\textup{aux}}}
from
{\mathbf{h}}_{\mathscr{P}}
, an
\mathsf{LPN}
sample
({{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}},\left\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\right\rangle)
which can be viewed as such by noticing that it is indeed equal to

(5)

\left({\mathbf{a}},\langle{\mathbf{a}},{\mathbf{s}}\rangle+e\right)\quad\mbox{with}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathbf{a}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}\\
{\mathbf{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}\\
e\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}+{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\rangle\end{array}\right.

Notice here that, if
{\mathcal{D}\mathrm{ec}}({\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}})
contains more than one element, we can compute such
\mathsf{LPN}
samples for each different
{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\in{\mathcal{D}\mathrm{ec}}({\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}})
.
The secret in the above
\mathsf{LPN}
sample is no longer given by
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}
that we want to recover (contrarily
to
\mathsf{RLPN}
-decoding
[
CDMT22
]
), but is given by
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{k_{\textup{aux}}}}
which are
{k_{\textup{aux}}}<s
linear equations involving the
s=\left|{\mathscr{P}}\right|
bits of the vector
{\mathbf{e}}
we are looking for.

The main advantage of our new technique is that we end up with an
\mathsf{LPN}
problem whose dimension of the secret has decreased from
s
to
{k_{\textup{aux}}}
. However, the noise has increased; let us describe how it behaves in the following paragraph.

3.2.
Estimating the New Noise

The error
e
in Equation (
5
) is biased toward zero and its bias is a function of
n,s,t
and
u,w,{t_{\textup{aux}}}
which are respectively

u\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\left|{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\right|,\quad w\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\left|{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\right|\quad\mbox{and}\quad{t_{\textup{aux}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}|{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}+{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}|.

In the following statement we compute the bias of
e
over all the possible LPN samples, that is we compute

\bias_{\left({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right){\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}}\widetilde{{\mathscr{H}}}}\left(\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},\mathbf{h}_{\textup{aux}}+{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\rangle\right)=\frac{1}{\left|\widetilde{{\mathscr{H}}}\right|}\sum_{\left({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)\in\widetilde{{\mathscr{H}}}}(-1)^{\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}+{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\rangle}

where
\widetilde{{\mathscr{H}}}
is defined by

Definition 7
.

(6)

\widetilde{{\mathscr{H}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\{\left({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)\in\mathcal{C}^{\perp}\times{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}\>:\>|{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}|=w\mbox{ and }|{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}+{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}|={t_{\textup{aux}}}\}.

It is tempting to conjecture that this bias is well approximated by the bias of a Bernoulli variable

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\left\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},\mathbf{e}_{\textup{aux}}\right\rangle+\left\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle
where
\mathbf{e}_{\textup{aux}}
and
{\mathbf{w}}

are respectively drawn uniformly at random in the Hamming spheres

\mathcal{S}_{{t_{\textup{aux}}}}^{s}
and
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{n-s}
.
The sum
\left\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},\mathbf{e}_{\textup{aux}}\right\rangle+\left\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle

is performed over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
and all the vectors are independent random variables. Because of the independence of the random variables, from the
straightforward fact that
\bias(X_{1}+X_{2})=\bias(X_{1})\bias(X_{2})
when
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are independent Bernoulli variables (and the addition is performed modulo
2
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\bias(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bias\left(\left\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\textup{aux}}\right\rangle\right)\bias\left(\left\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{w}}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{K_{{t_{\textup{aux}}}}^{(s)}(t-u)}{\binom{s}{{t_{\textup{aux}}}}}\frac{K_{w}^{(n-s)}(u)}{\binom{n-s}{w}}\qquad\text{(by Fact \ref{fact:krawtchouk_bias})}.

This kind of approximation was done in the early days of statistical decoding
[
Jab01
,
Ove06
,
DT17c
]
, until
[
CDMT22
, Prop. 3.1]
which has shown that under certain conditions,
i.e.
when there are enough available parity-check equations of weight
w
(essentially when the number is of order
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\omega\mathopen{}\left(1/\delta^{2}\right)
where
\delta
is the bias), then this approximation can indeed be shown to hold with overwhelming probability. It turns out that
[
CDMT22
, Prop. 3.1]
can be adapted to our setting with some additional technicalities and conditions. It can be shown that with overwhelming probability we indeed have

\bias_{\left({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right){\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}}\widetilde{{\mathscr{H}}}}\left(\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},\mathbf{h}_{{\mathscr{P}}}+\mathbf{c}_{\textup{aux}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\rangle\right)=(1+o(1))\bias(X).

This is in essence what the following proposition shows.

Proposition 2
.

Suppose that the parameters are such that for some constant
\alpha>0

(7)

\frac{\binom{n-s}{w}\binom{s}{{t_{\textup{aux}}}}}{2^{k-{k_{\textup{aux}}}}}=\mathop{}\mathopen{}\omega\mathopen{}\left(\frac{n^{\alpha}}{\delta^{2}}\right)\quad\mbox{ where }\quad\delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{K_{w}^{(n-s)}\left(u\right)K_{{t_{\textup{aux}}}}^{(s)}(t-u)}{\binom{n-s}{w}\binom{s}{{t_{\textup{aux}}}}}.

Moreover suppose that

(8)

\frac{\binom{n-s}{w}\binom{s}{{t_{\textup{aux}}}}}{2^{k}}=\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(n^{\alpha}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{\binom{s}{{t_{\textup{aux}}}}}{2^{s-{k_{\textup{aux}}}}}=\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(n^{\alpha}\right).

Let
{\mathscr{N}}
be a set of
n-s
positions in
\left\llbracket 1,n\right\rrbracket
and
{\mathscr{P}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\backslash{\mathscr{N}}
. Let
{\mathbf{e}}
be a vector of weight
u
on
{\mathscr{N}}
and
t-u
on
{\mathscr{P}}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
and
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
be
[n,k]
and
[s,{k_{\textup{aux}}}]
linear codes respectively.
Let us choose
({{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}},{\mathbf{h}})
uniformly at random in

\widetilde{{\mathscr{H}}}=\{\left({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)\in\mathcal{C}^{\perp}\times{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}\>:\>|{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}|=w\mbox{ and }|{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}+{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}|={t_{\textup{aux}}}\}.

Then for a proportion
1-o(1)
of codes
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
and
\mathcal{C}
we have that

\bias_{\left({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right){\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}}\widetilde{{\mathscr{H}}}}\left(\langle{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}+{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}},{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}},{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}\rangle\right)=\delta(1+o(1)).

Proof.

See Appendix §
A
.
∎

4.
The
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
Algorithm

We first going to explain the four main ingredients of the
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
algorithm:

•

computing suitable LPN samples,

•

FFT decoding,

•

recovering
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}
,

•

the bet ensuring that there are
u
errors on
{\mathscr{N}}
at some point.

Let us detail each of these ingredients (or steps of the algorithm).

Computing the LPN Samples.

First, our algorithm computes a certain number of LPN samples by computing a set
{\mathscr{W}}
of elements of
\mathcal{C}^{\perp}
of weight
w
on
{\mathscr{N}}
by using a procedure
ParityCheckEquations
(w,{\mathscr{N}},\mathcal{C})
that uses low-weight codewords search techniques to produce a bunch of parity-check equations of
\mathcal{C}
of weight
w
on
{\mathscr{N}}
. Then a random code
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
is chosen in a family of codes over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\left|{\mathscr{P}}\right|}
and dimension
{k_{\textup{aux}}}
that we know how to decode efficiently at distance
{t_{\textup{aux}}}
.
For an element
{\mathbf{h}}
in
{\mathscr{W}}
, each
{\mathbf{h}}_{\mathscr{P}}
is decoded at distance
{t_{\textup{aux}}}
to finally compute the set
{\mathscr{H}}
containing pairs
\left({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)
in
{\mathscr{W}}\times{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
satisfying
|{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}|=w
and
|{\mathbf{h}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}+{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}|={t_{\textup{aux}}}
.
Algorithm
1
gives the pseudo-code of the procedure.

1:

function

LPN-Samples
(
\mathcal{C},{\mathscr{N}}
)

2:

{\mathscr{P}}\leftarrow\left\llbracket 1,n\right\rrbracket\setminus{\mathscr{N}}

3:

{\mathscr{W}}\leftarrow\textsc{ParityCheckEquations}(w,{\mathscr{N}},\mathcal{C})

\triangleright
returns a set of parity-check equations of
\mathcal{C}
of weight
w
on
{\mathscr{N}}

4:

{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}}{\mathscr{F}}({\mathscr{P}},{k_{\textup{aux}}},{t_{\textup{aux}}})

\triangleright
returns a code
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
in a family of codes
{\mathscr{F}}
over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\left|{\mathscr{P}}\right|}
and dimension
{k_{\textup{aux}}}
that we know how to decode efficiently at distance
{t_{\textup{aux}}}

5:

{\mathscr{H}}\leftarrow\emptyset

6:

for all

{\mathbf{h}}\in{\mathscr{H}}

do

7:

{\mathscr{H}}\leftarrow{\mathscr{H}}\cup\{{\mathbf{h}}\}\times\textsc{Decode}({\mathbf{h}}_{\mathscr{P}},{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}},{t_{\textup{aux}}})

\triangleright

\textsc{Decode}({\mathbf{h}}_{\mathscr{P}},{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}},{t_{\textup{aux}}})
outputs a set of codewords of
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
at distance
{t_{\textup{aux}}}
of
{\mathbf{h}}_{\mathscr{P}}

8:

end

for

9:

{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}\leftarrow
generating matrix of
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}

10:

return

\left({\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)

11:

end

function

Algorithm 1

The function computing the LPN samples associated to
{\mathscr{N}}

FFT Decoding.

Computing
{\mathscr{H}}
gives a number
\left|{\mathscr{H}}\right|
of
\mathsf{LPN}
samples, which from the interpretation given in Equation (
5
), leads us to think that
the right choice
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\textup{aux}}}
for
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
is the one for which

\bias_{({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}){\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}}{\mathscr{H}}}\left(\left\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\right\rangle+\left\langle{\mathbf{x}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle\right)

would be given by Proposition
2
. It should namely be of order
\delta
which is defined in this proposition. Natural candidates for being equal to
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
are those for which this bias is say
\geqslant\delta/2
. This leads to compute all those biases. This can be done rather efficiently by factoring the common
computations made for computing all those biases for
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\textup{aux}}}
by an FFT trick which is standard in the LPN context. It dates back in this context to
[
LF06
]
, but it can be traced back to decoding the first-order Reed-Muller code (which is another way to view the decoding task
in case of the LPN problem) which was already suggested in
[
Gre66
]
.
The link between the bias of the random variables we are interested in and the Fourier transform is based on the following simple observation that follows right away from the very definition of the Fourier transform. Before we give this observation, let us bring in a notation that will be helpful for describing it and which will be used throughout the paper from now on.

Notation 2
.

For any
{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
,
{\mathscr{H}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\perp}\times{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
and a generator matrix
{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}
of
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
we define the function

f_{{\mathbf{y}},{\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}}
on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\textup{aux}}}
by

\displaystyle f_{{\mathbf{y}},{\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{k_{\textup{aux}}}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\sum_{{\mathbf{h}}:\left({\mathbf{h}},\mathbf{u}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)\in{\mathscr{H}}}(-1)^{\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\rangle}&\text{if this sum is not empty,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

With this notation at hand, the link between the biases and the Fourier transform of this function is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 1
.

We have for any
{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\textup{aux}}}
and any
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s}
such that
{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}={\mathbf{u}}

\displaystyle\widehat{f_{{\mathbf{y}},{\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}}}\left({\mathbf{u}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|{\mathscr{H}}\right|\;\bias_{({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}){\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}}{\mathscr{H}}}(\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{u}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|{\mathscr{H}}\right|\;\bias_{({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}){\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}}{\mathscr{H}}}(\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{x}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle).

Proof.

We have the following computation,

\displaystyle\widehat{f_{{\mathbf{y}},{\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}}}\left({\mathbf{u}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\textup{aux}}}}(-1)^{\left\langle{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\right\rangle}f_{{\mathbf{y}},{\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}}({\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{({\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{v}})\in\mathcal{C}^{\perp}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\textup{aux}}}:\left({\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{v}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)\in{\mathscr{H}}}(-1)^{\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{u}},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle}

(Equations (
2
) and (
2
))

\displaystyle=\sum_{({\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{v}})\in\mathcal{C}^{\perp}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\textup{aux}}}:\left({\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{v}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)\in{\mathscr{H}}}(-1)^{\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}},{\mathbf{v}}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{({\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{v}})\in\mathcal{C}^{\perp}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\textup{aux}}}:\left({\mathbf{h}},{\mathbf{v}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)\in{\mathscr{H}}}(-1)^{\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{v}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)\in{\mathscr{H}}}(-1)^{\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{x}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle}

which concludes the proof by definition of the bias.
∎

Remark 2
.

The probabilistic notation hides the fact that computing all these Fourier coefficients and taking the maximum of them allows to decode in a certain code.
Indeed let,

\mathcal{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\left\{(\left\langle{\mathbf{u}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle)_{({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}})\in{\mathscr{H}}}:{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k_{\textup{aux}}}\right\}

which is under very mild assumptions a linear code of dimension
{k_{\textup{aux}}}
and length
\left|{\mathscr{H}}\right|
. If we let
c({\mathbf{u}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}(\left\langle{\mathbf{u}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}\right\rangle)_{({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}})\in{\mathscr{H}}}
be the codeword associated to
{\mathbf{u}}
and
{\mathbf{v}}=(\left\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\right\rangle)_{({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}})\in{\mathscr{H}}}
then since

\left|{\mathscr{H}}\right|\;\bias_{({\mathbf{h}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}){\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}}{\mathscr{H}}}(\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{u}},{{\mathbf{m}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle)=\left|{\mathscr{H}}\right|-2|{\mathbf{v}}+c({\mathbf{u}})|,

it follows from Lemma
1
that
c({\mathbf{u}}_{0})
is the codeword of
\mathcal{D}
which is the closest to
{\mathbf{v}}
, where
{\mathbf{u}}_{0}=\arg\max\widehat{f_{{\mathbf{y}},{\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}}}\left({\mathbf{u}}\right)
. Therefore, vector
{\mathbf{u}}_{0}
is here a likely candidate for being equal to
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
when
{\mathscr{H}}
is big enough.

We give the pseudo-code of the FFT decoding algorithm producing a list
{\mathscr{S}}
of putative candidates for being equal to
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}

in Algorithm
2
.

Algorithm 2

FFT algorithm producing a list of candidates for
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}

Input:

{\mathscr{H}}
,
{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}

Output:

{\mathscr{S}}
a list of candidates for
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}

1:

function

FFT-Decode
(
{\mathscr{H}},\;{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}
)

2:

\widehat{f_{{\mathbf{y}},{\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}}}\leftarrow
FFT
(
f_{{\mathbf{y}},{\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}}
)

3:

{\mathscr{S}}\leftarrow\left\{{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{k_{\textup{aux}}}}\>:\widehat{f_{{\mathbf{y}},{\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}}}({\mathbf{u}})>\frac{\delta}{2}\left|{\mathscr{H}}\right|\right\}

\triangleright

\delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{K_{w}^{(n-s)}(u)K_{{t_{\textup{aux}}}}^{(s)}(t-u)}{\binom{n-s}{w}\binom{s}{{t_{\textup{aux}}}}}

4:

return

{\mathscr{S}}

5:

end

function

The point of using the FFT for computing all these biases is that its complexity is of order
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left({k_{\textup{aux}}}2^{k_{\textup{aux}}}F\right)
where
F
is the complexity of computing
f_{{\mathbf{y}},{\mathscr{H}},{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}}
which can be bounded by
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(\max\left(1,\frac{\left|{\mathscr{H}}\right|}{2^{k_{\textup{aux}}}}\right)\right)
. On the other hand, if we had computed directly all those biases we would have a much bigger complexity of
\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(\left|{\mathscr{H}}\right|2^{k_{\textup{aux}}}\right)
because
{\mathscr{H}}
is of exponential size for the problem at hand.

Recovering
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}
and then
{\mathbf{e}}
.

If we have a candidate
{{\mathbf{s}}}
for
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
, then since we expect
|{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}|=t-u
, recovering
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}
from the equality
{{\mathbf{s}}}={\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
is nothing but solving a decoding problem, namely to decode
t-u
errors in the code of parity-check matrix
{{\mathbf{G}}_{\textup{aux}}}
,
i.e.
\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}^{\perp}
. In other words, we have to solve
\textsf{DP}(s,s-{k_{\textup{aux}}},t-u)
.
This approach can be generalized by taking
{N}_{\textup{aux}}
different sets of
\mathsf{LPN}
samples associated respectively to the codes
{\mathcal{C}^{(1)}_{\textup{aux}}},\cdots,{\mathcal{C}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})}_{\textup{aux}}}
. For
i
in
\left\llbracket 1,{N}_{\textup{aux}}\right\rrbracket
, let
{{\mathbf{G}}^{(i)}_{\textup{aux}}}
be the generating matrix which is chosen for
{\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{\textup{aux}}}
. Then each of these sets of
\mathsf{LPN}
samples brings candidates for
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
. By choosing an
{N}_{\textup{aux}}
-tuple of candidates
({\mathbf{s}}^{(1)},\cdots,{\mathbf{s}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})})
, where
{\mathbf{s}}^{(i)}
is a candidate for
{{\mathbf{G}}^{(i)}_{\textup{aux}}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}^{\intercal}
(we have taken the transpose to have a more readable form) given by the
i
-th
\mathsf{LPN}
samples set, we get to solve the
set of simultaneous equations

({\mathbf{s}}^{(1)})^{\intercal}={{\mathbf{G}}^{(1)}_{\textup{aux}}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}^{\intercal},\cdots,\;({\mathbf{s}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})})^{\intercal}={{\mathbf{G}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})}_{\textup{aux}}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}^{\intercal}

with the constraint
|{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}|=t-u
. In other words if we set

{\mathbf{H}}^{\intercal}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}{{\mathbf{G}}^{(1)}_{\textup{aux}}}^{\intercal}&\cdots&{{\mathbf{G}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})}_{\textup{aux}}}^{\intercal}\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathbf{s}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}&\ldots&{\mathbf{s}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})}\end{pmatrix}

then we have to solve the decoding problem
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}^{\intercal}={\mathbf{s}}^{\intercal}
with
|{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}|=t-u
, in other words we have to solve

\textsf{DP}\left(s,s-{N}_{\textup{aux}}{k_{\textup{aux}}},t-u\right)
. We are going to choose a simple ISD algorithm to solve this problem, namely
Dumer’s algorithm
[
Dum89
]
which is a good compromise between efficiency and simple formula for its complexity. We denote by

Decode-Dumer
(
{\mathbf{H}},{\mathbf{s}},t
) the call to Dumer’s algorithm to decode the syndrome
{\mathbf{s}}
of an error of weight
t
associated to the
parity-check matrix
{\mathbf{H}}
. We assume here that this call produces
all
solutions to this decoding problem.

Once we have recovered
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}
, say we know that it is equal to some
{\mathbf{v}}
of weight
t-u
in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s}
, we face a much simpler problem. We namely have to solve the problem

{\mathbf{y}}={\mathbf{c}}+{\mathbf{e}},\;{\mathbf{c}}\in\mathcal{C},\;{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}={\mathbf{v}},\;|{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{N}}|=u.

This is nothing but
\textsf{DP}(n-s,k-s,u)
which is much simpler. Here we might just use algorithm
Decode-Dumer
on it.
Let us call
\textsc{Solve-SubProblem}(\mathcal{C},{\mathscr{N}},{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{v}},u)
the routine which performs this task and which returns a candidate for
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{N}}

and returns
\bot
otherwise.
If this problem has no solution we have of course a false candidate for
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}
and if we have a solution, then we have solved our decoding problem. To verify that we have indeed such a decoding problem, suppose without loss of generality that
{\mathscr{P}}=\llbracket 1,s\rrbracket
and
{\mathscr{N}}=\llbracket s+1,n\rrbracket
. We can also assume that
\mathcal{C}_{{\mathscr{P}}}
is of full rank dimension
s
(this holds with overwhelming probability). We can compute
{\mathbf{G}}
a generator matrix of
\mathcal{C}
of the form
{\mathbf{G}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Id}_{s}&{\mathbf{R}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{k-s}&{\mathbf{R}}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

by applying partial Gaussian elimination on a generator matrix of
\mathcal{C}
.
Then
\textsc{Solve-SubProblem}(\mathcal{C},{\mathscr{N}},{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{x}},u)
decodes at distance
u
the word
{\mathbf{y}}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathbf{y}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}-({\mathbf{y}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}-{\mathbf{x}}){\mathbf{R}}
onto the code
\mathcal{C}^{{\mathscr{N}}}
of generator matrix
{\mathbf{R}}^{\prime}
.

With this notation at hand, the pseudo-code describing the algorithm for recovering
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}
and then returning
{\mathbf{e}}
if a suitable solution is found, is given in Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

algorithm recovering
{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{P}}
and then
{\mathbf{e}}

Input:

{\mathscr{S}}^{(1)},\cdots,{\mathscr{S}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})}\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{k_{\textup{aux}}}}
,
{{\mathbf{G}}^{(1)}_{\textup{aux}}},\cdots,{{\mathbf{G}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})}_{\textup{aux}}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{k_{\textup{aux}}}\times s}

1:

function

Recover-
{\mathbf{e}}
(
{\mathscr{S}}^{(1)},\cdots,{\mathscr{S}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})},{{\mathbf{G}}^{(1)}_{\textup{aux}}},\cdots,{{\mathbf{G}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})}_{\textup{aux}}}
)

2:

{\mathbf{H}}^{\intercal}\leftarrow\begin{pmatrix}{{\mathbf{G}}^{(1)}_{\textup{aux}}}^{\intercal}&\cdots&{{\mathbf{G}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})}_{\textup{aux}}}^{\intercal}\end{pmatrix}

3:

for

\left({\mathbf{s}}^{(1)},...,{\mathbf{s}}^{(N_{\textup{aux}})}\right)\in\prod_{j=1}^{N_{\textup{aux}}}\mathcal{S}^{(j)}

do

4:

{\mathbf{s}}\leftarrow\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}&\ldots&{\mathbf{s}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})}\end{pmatrix}

5:

for all

{\mathbf{v}}\in\textsc{Decode-Dumer}({\mathbf{H}},{\mathbf{s}},t)

do

6:

{\mathbf{e}}^{\prime}\leftarrow\textsc{Solve-SubProblem}(\mathcal{C},{\mathscr{N}},{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{v}},u)

7:

if

{\mathbf{e}}^{\prime}\neq\bot

then

8:

return

{\mathbf{e}}
such that
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{P}}}={\mathbf{x}}
and
{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}={\mathbf{e}}^{\prime}

9:

end

if

10:

end

for

11:

end

for

12:

end

function

Testing Enough Candidates
{\mathscr{N}}
.

Now, it may also happen that when choosing
{\mathscr{N}}
, we might not have that
|{\mathbf{e}}_{\mathscr{N}}|=u
. For this, we have to check enough candidates. The probability that a set
{\mathscr{N}}
of size
n-s
satisfies this property is given by

{P}_{\textup{succ}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{\binom{t}{u}\binom{n-t}{n-u-s}}{\binom{n}{n-s}}.

Performing a number
{N}_{\textup{iter}}
of trials for
{\mathscr{N}}
which is of order
\Theta\left(1/{P}_{\textup{succ}}\right)
will succeed with constant probability.
Putting all these ingredients together leads to the whole
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
algorithm given in Algorithm
4
.

Algorithm 4

\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
decoder

Input:

{\mathbf{y}}
,
t
,
\mathcal{C}
an
[n,k]
-code

Parameters:

s,u,{k_{\textup{aux}}},{t_{\textup{aux}}},N,\left({\mathcal{C}^{(j)}_{\textup{aux}}}\right)_{j\in\mathcal{F}}

Output:

{\mathbf{e}}
such that
|{\mathbf{e}}|=t
and
{\mathbf{y}}-{\mathbf{e}}\in\mathcal{C}
.

1:

function

\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
(
{\mathbf{y}}
,
\mathcal{C}
,
t
)

2:

for

i
from
1
to
{N}_{\textup{iter}}

do

\triangleright

{N}_{\textup{iter}}
such that w.o.p one iteration is s.t
|{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}|=u

3:

{\mathscr{N}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\left\{{\mathscr{I}}\subseteq\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\ :\ \left|{\mathscr{I}}\right|=n-s\right\}

\triangleright
Hope that
|{\mathbf{e}}_{{\mathscr{N}}}|=u

4:

for

j=1,\dots,N_{\textup{aux}}

do

5:

({\mathscr{H}}^{(j)},{{\mathbf{G}}^{(j)}_{\textup{aux}}})\leftarrow\textsc{LPN-Samples}(\mathcal{C},{\mathscr{N}})

6:

\mathcal{S}^{(j)}\leftarrow\textsc{FFT-Decode}({\mathscr{H}}^{(j)},{{\mathbf{G}}^{(j)}_{\textup{aux}}})

7:

end

for

8:

Recover-
{\mathbf{e}}
(
{\mathscr{S}}^{(1)},\cdots,{\mathscr{S}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})},{{\mathbf{G}}^{(1)}_{\textup{aux}}},\cdots,{{\mathbf{G}}^{({N}_{\textup{aux}})}_{\textup{aux}}}
)

9:

end

for

10:

end

function

Complexity of the Algorithm.

It is sufficient to take
{N}_{\textup{aux}}=\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\left(1\right)
for the parameters we are interested in which will correspond to a choice of
{k_{\textup{aux}}}
of the form

{k_{\textup{aux}}}=\Omega\left(s\right)
. With this choice we immediately get the following complexity for the
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
algorithm

Proposition 3
.

The complexity
C
of the
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
algorithm is given by

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{}\mathopen{}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\mathopen{}\left(\frac{1}{{P}_{\textup{succ}}}\left({T}_{\textup{eq}}+N{T}_{\textup{dec}}+{k_{\textup{aux}}}\max\left(2^{k_{\textup{aux}}},\left|{\mathscr{H}}\right|\right)+S^{{N}_{\textup{aux}}}{T}_{\textup{ISD}}\right)\right)\;\;\text{where}

\displaystyle{P}_{\textup{succ}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\binom{t}{u}\binom{n-t}{n-u-s}}{\binom{n}{n-s}}

\displaystyle{T}_{\textup{ISD}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{T}_{\textup{Dumer}}(s,s-{N}_{\textup{aux}}{k_{\textup{aux}}},t-u)+{N}_{\textup{ISD}}{T}_{\textup{Dumer}}(n-s,k-s,u)

and
{T}_{\textup{eq}}
is the time complexity of
ParityCheckEquations
,

N
is the number of parity-check equations produced by this procedure,
{T}_{\textup{dec}}
is the complexity of
decoding
{\mathcal{C}_{\textup{aux}}}
, i.e. it is the complexity of a call to
\textsc{Decode}(
),
S
is the size of a list output by

\textsc{FFT-Decode}(
),
{N}_{\textup{ISD}}
is the number of solutions to the
(s,s-{N}_{\textup{aux}}{k_{\textup{aux}}},t-u)
decoding problem and
{T}_{\textup{Dumer}}(n,k,t)
stands for the complexity of solving the
(n,k,t)
decoding problem with Dumer’s algorithm when we want to find all solutions to the problem.

The asymptotic complexity formula for the
\mathsf{double}
-
\mathsf{RLPN}
algorithm, including also the constraints required on our parameters, is given in Appendix
B
Proposition
9
which was used to generate Figure
1
.

5.
Estimating the Number of False Candidates

The goal of this section is to introduce the main tool necessary to make a rigorous analysis of Algorithm
4
and to
give a formula for the number of false candidates which is proved by making a certain conjecture whose validity has then been verified experimentally.

5.1.
Main Duality Tool

The fundamental quantity when analyzing dual attacks is the bias of
\langle{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{h}}\rangle+\langle{\mathbf{x}},{{\mathbf{c}}_{\textup{aux}}}\rangle
which tells us whether
{\mathbf{x}}{{\mathbf{G}}^{\intercal}_{\textup{aux}}}
has to be put in the list
{\mathscr{S}}
of candidates output by Algorithm
2
. While initially standard independence assumptions were made to analyze its distribution
[
CDMT22
, Ass. 3.7]
(which are very similar to analyze dual attacks in lattice based cryptography), recently
[
MT23
]
showed that these assumptions were erroneous and, gave for the first time a dual expression
[
MT23
, Prop 1.]
for this quantity which seems a key step to understand its behavior and gave with an additional assumption a rigorous analysis of the
\mathsf{RLPN}
dual attack. The proposition given there to estimate the number of false candidates turns out to match accurately the experiments.
The following proposition is a generalization of
[
MT23
, Prop 1.]
